- `ProviderAccounts.BuiltIn` is used only when the caller passes it (the app and `odyssey-scrape` do; tests never see a release's), and then it wins: a `dev_id` or `dev_password` in `secrets.toml` or the environment is ignored, with an Info diagnostic (not a problem in the settings screen). Its values are in `ProviderAccounts.Values`, so `Redactor` masks them too. The settings screen doesn't list developer credentials in a release, only the user's account; a build from source lists them, after the account. `BuiltInAccountsTests` checks, in the release workflow, that the generated file was compiled in.
- A provider without its credentials is skipped, and the message says which keys or variables to set and where. Diagnostics about the file never quote a value, and a syntax error shows only its position.
- ScreenScraper embeds the credentials in every URL, and echoes them back in each response (`header.commandRequested`, every media URL). `Redactor` masks them in saved responses and logs; a test scans every file the scrape writes, and the log, for every credential.
- IGDB's app access token (about 60 days, and a Twitch application may only have 25) is cached in `DataDir/tokens/igdb.json`, keyed by a hash of the client id, and replaced a day before expiry or when IGDB refuses it. The client secret travels in a form body, never a URL.
- The settings screen (M7) writes credentials to `secrets.toml` and nowhere else, and never shows one: each is masked, with where it's set (the file, or an `ODYSSEY_*` variable, which wins); the on-screen keyboard starts empty and types hidden. "Test connection" builds a scrape service of its own from `secrets.toml` as saved, so a refusal doesn't rest the provider for real scrapes.

## A6. Theme manifest (`themes/<id>/theme.toml`)

A theme is a folder with a `theme.toml` and the models it names. The guide for theme authors (Blender, testing) is [THEMING.md](THEMING.md); the sample theme is [`samples/themes/retro-tv/`](../samples/themes/retro-tv/theme.toml). Paths are relative to the folder, `/`-separated, and can't leave it. The built-in theme is [`godot/themes/memory-card/theme.toml`](../godot/themes/memory-card/theme.toml); the M6 test theme is [`tests/themes/slot-showcase/theme.toml`](../tests/themes/slot-showcase/theme.toml).

```toml
format = 1
name = "Memory Card"
author = "Odyssey Launcher"
look_transition_ms = 300             # how long a cross-fade between looks takes; default 300, 0 to 5000

[look.background]                    # icon.sys-style: one sRGB colour per screen corner
top_left     = "#1B1F4A"
top_right    = "#1B1F4A"
bottom_left  = "#04040C"
bottom_right = "#0B0B24"
wallpaper    = "wallpapers/main.webp" # optional (2026-10-18): a PNG, JPEG or WebP over the corners, at most 16 MB
fit          = "cover"               # optional: "cover" (fill, cropped evenly; the default) or "stretch"

[look.ambient]
colour = "#303038"
energy = 1.0                         # default 1

[[look.lights]]                      # 1-3 directional lights, as in icon.sys
direction = [-0.5, -0.4, -0.75]      # direction the light travels, in view space: +X right, +Y up, +Z towards the viewer
colour = "#FFFFFF"                   # default white
energy = 1.0                         # default 1

[defaults]
system_model = "models/systems/generic.glb"   # the card for systems without their own model
tint_system_model = true                      # its plain materials take each system's colour
game_template = "dvd_case"                    # for systems the theme doesn't assign one

[templates.dvd_case]                 # a game template: a model, and optional slot chains
model = "models/templates/dvd_case.glb"
shape = "model"                      # optional: "model" (its own, the default) or "media" (each game's art: A7)
video = "screenshot"                 # optional (2026-10-11): the slot whose face plays the focused game's video, with its sound
video_delay_ms = 1000                # optional (2026-10-11): how long the game is focused before it starts (0 to 60000, default 1000)

[templates.dvd_case.slots]           # optional: a slot the table doesn't list is [<its kind>, "generated"]
back = ["back", "screenshot", "generated"]

[templates.dvd_case.fit]             # optional, per slot: "crop" (fill the face, the default) or "whole" (fitted inside it)
back = "crop"

[defaults.system_slots]              # optional (2026-10-04): the chains of this theme's cards' slots
label = ["logo", "generated"]        # a card can name only "logo" (its system's image), "generated" and "authored"

[systems.saturn]
model = "models/systems/saturn.glb"  # optional: the system's card (default: [defaults] system_model)
tint = false                         # optional: default false for its own model, else tint_system_model
colour = "#4A4F5C"                   # optional: its card's tint and its plain boxes' colour
game_template = "jewel_case"         # optional: a template id in this theme (default: [defaults] game_template)
logo = "art/saturn.png"              # optional (2026-10-04): its card's image (default: logos/saturn.png, .jpg, .jpeg or .webp)
video = "videos/saturn.mp4"          # optional (2026-10-11): an MP4 the card's model plays on its `video` material while focused

[systems.saturn.slots]               # optional (2026-10-04): its card's chains, over [defaults.system_slots]
cover = ["logo", "authored"]

[systems.saturn.look.background]     # optional: the look while its games are shown
top_left = "#2A1A3A"
top_right = "#2A1A3A"
bottom_left = "#050008"
bottom_right = "#100818"

[ui.menu]                            # optional (2026-10-15): the menus' look; each key left out is the base's, else the launcher's own
background = "#141414"               # the panels; their rows, frames, borders and the dimming behind them are mixed from it
text = "#ECECEC"                     # the text; the dim and faint text are mixed from it towards background
accent = "#C8C8C8"                   # the focused row's fill is mixed from it; values and row actions
font = "fonts/menu.ttf"              # a TTF, OTF, WOFF or WOFF2 inside the folder, at most 4 MB

[motion]                             # optional (2026-10-15): the grids' procedural motion, as multiples of the launcher's own
focus_sway = 1.0                     # the focused item's sway; 0 to 3, default the base's (1); 0 faces the camera
idle_bob = 1.0                       # how far the items not focused bob; 0 to 3, default the base's (1); 0 holds them still
```

- **Colours:** `#RRGGBB` sRGB. `energy` is a linear multiplier, 0 to 16.
- **Background:**
  - The four corners interpolate in sRGB, as on the PS2.
  - The gradient is drawn on a background canvas layer rather than a sky, which avoids radiance-map and cubemap-pass problems and makes cross-fades free.
  - With the Linear tonemapper, glow off and ambient source Colour, each corner pixel renders as its exact hex value **on Forward+**. On the Mobile renderer (the one we use, M1) the canvas background is composited through the 3D buffer, whose precision moves dark corners by up to 2/255 (M6 measured `#04040C` as `#06060D`); Forward+ gives the hex values exactly ([perf/m6-themes.md](perf/m6-themes.md#corner-colours)).
  - Reflections are disabled.
  - **Wallpapers (2026-10-18):** a background block's `wallpaper` (a PNG, JPEG or WebP inside the folder, at most `LookBackground.MaxWallpaperBytes`, 16 MB; a bad one is an error at its key and the block keeps its corners) is drawn over the corners by the same canvas layer, its alpha showing them; `fit` is `cover` (cropped evenly to the screen's aspect, worked out in the shader from the viewport's size) or `stretch`. The path is stored absolute, so a block inherited from the base theme keeps the base's file. It's part of the block, so a system's background replaces the theme's wallpaper too, as it does the corners.
    - **Loading:** `Theming/Wallpapers` decodes each on the thread pool (Godot's decoder, whose image is native memory freed at once, where the OS decoder's managed buffer would sit on the large-object heap: `GodotWork`), scaled to fit 2560 × 1080 (the background canvas goes through the 3D buffer, A3, which is at most 1080 high, so a bigger image costs memory and adds no detail), and makes its texture there, so the main thread never decodes or uploads one. At most three stay loaded (about 8 MB each at 1920 × 1080: the theme's, the shown system's, one fetched ahead), least recently used first, never one on screen; a theme applied (switched to, or reloaded) lets go of those not on screen. Boot asks for the theme's as soon as the theme is resolved, in parallel with the DB (never waited for: the corners show until it's in, then it fades in over 0.3 s); a system card focused for 0.15 s fetches its system's (`Navigator.PrefetchWallpaper`), so entering it usually finds it loaded.
    - **Drawing:** `LookStage` keeps two slots, each a texture and its aspect, and blends their weights with the corners in `State` (a look shows one, so the weights never sum past 1): the layer draws `gradient·(1 − a − b) + A·a + B·b`, `a` and `b` each slot's weight times its texel's alpha, so a cross-fade between two wallpapers, or to or from none, is one more lerp and no allocation. The slot showing less takes a new wallpaper. The item shader repeats the sum (`backdrop()`) so items fade exactly into it (A3), but only under `grid_fade > 0`, a uniform branch: at rest it reads nothing. A material's textures are set only when a slot's texture changes (`LookBackdrop.SameWallpapers`), since pointing a material at a texture remakes its uniform set; the weights are floats each fade frame, as the corners were. A system's details' `ModelView` has no `Wallpapers`, so it shows the corners alone.
    - **Costs**, on the export ([perf/wallpapers.md](perf/wallpapers.md)): see the decisions log, 2026-10-18.
- **Lights:**
  - Directions are in **view space**, as in icon.sys, so the three `DirectionalLight3D`s are children of the camera.
  - Any non-zero direction is valid, including straight up and down.
  - Lights the theme doesn't define are switched off.
- **Per-system looks:**
  - Entering a system cross-fades to its look over `look_transition_ms`, and leaving cross-fades back; the systems grid, Favourites, Recently played and All games use the theme's own look. Mid-fade, the lights' directions, colours and energies blend, and a light only one side has fades from or to energy 0.
  - A per-system `background`, `ambient` or `lights` block **replaces** the theme's block whole. A background needs all four corners.
  - Anything left unspecified falls back to the theme's look; a theme's own blocks fall back to the base theme's. A system the active theme gives no look keeps the theme's look, not the base's look for that system (the owner's choice, 2026-10-03).
  - The base theme gives each of the original 14 built-in systems a colour, a template and its own background and lights (generated from its colour), and each of the 150 ES-DE catalogue systems, and Windows, Steam and Desktop Apps, a colour (by manufacturer) and the nearest of the boxes (DOS the `generic_box_spine`, and Windows and Steam the `generic_box_logo`, from 2026-10-02, and Desktop Apps from 2026-10-03; N64 the `generic_box_top_spine`, from 2026-10-03, and SNES the `snes_box_horizontal`, from 2026-10-04) or, for the arcade systems, the `arcade_cabinet` (from 2026-10-02), with the theme's look. A regional variant (A5, from 2026-11-02) has its system's colour and look and its regions' box: Dreamcast (NA/JP) the `jewel_case`, while Dreamcast has the `dreamcast_jewel_case`.
- **Templates** (`[templates.<id>]`): a game template is a `.glb` (any slots, or none: M6 part 2 dropped the `cover` requirement, so a template can be a television showing the screenshot) and optional slot chains (A7 Media slots). From 2026-10-01 `shape = "media"` makes each game's box take its proportions from its cover and its depth from its spine (A7 Boxes shaped by the art); a bad value is an error and the template keeps its own shape, and a model with no `cover` material takes its proportions from its `screenshot` instead (from 2026-10-07: a flat screenshot card), or with neither keeps its own with a warning. From 2026-10-02 `[templates.<id>.fit]` sets a slot to `"whole"`: its art is fitted inside the face at its own proportions over the slot's fallback, never cropped (A7 Art drawn whole); a bad value or slot is an error and the slot stays cropped, and a slot the model lacks a warning. Themes name their templates and assign them to systems; the resolution order is in A7.
- **System cards' logos (2026-10-04):** a theme can ship one image per system and show it on its cards, so one card model serves every system. A card's image (its `logo`, the only media a system has) is `[systems.<id>] logo` (a PNG, JPEG or WebP inside the folder: a bad one is an error at its key), else `logos/<id>.png`, `.jpg`, `.jpeg` or `.webp` (in that order; the file name is the id, matched ignoring case), else the base theme's (which has none); Favourites, Recently played and All games use the ids `favourites`, `recently_played` and `all_games`. Cards' slots take chains like templates': `[defaults.system_slots]`, and a system's `[systems.<id>.slots]` over it, slot by slot. A card chain may name only `logo` of the media kinds, as a system has no other art (any other is an error, with "did you mean"); a slot no chain lists is `generated` where the launcher can draw it, else `authored`, as before. A card's chains are its own theme's (the theme whose model it is), and a user's own card takes the active theme's, so it shows the theme's logos too. A logo is drawn whole with a margin over the slot's fallback, as on a game's slot (A7 Art drawn whole); over `authored` it isn't multiplied by the material's colour, so a dark slot shows it on the card's own colour. The Slab theme (built in) is one dark grey card with `label = ["logo", "authored"]` and 169 images.
- **Card videos (2026-10-11):** a system card can play a video while it's focused. `[systems.<id>] video` is an MP4 inside the theme's folder (a bad one is an error at its key); it belongs to the `model` of the same entry (one without a `model` is a warning, and ignored), so `ModelResolver` puts it on that candidate only (`ModelCandidate.Video`, part of its key: the same model with and without a video is two grid templates), never on a default card or another theme's. The model shows it on its material named `video` (matched as slot names are; not a slot), whose own base colour texture is what the card shows otherwise: `ModelConverter` records which authored texture that is (`ItemTemplate.VideoTexture`), and while the video plays the grid points that `authored_N` parameter of the card's material at the video's texture, then puts the texture back, so the item shader is unchanged. The systems grid plays only its focused card's video, only while the grid shows and nothing covers it (`ItemGrid.UpdateVideo`, once a frame, starting, ticking or stopping it; from 2026-10-27 held off by `VideosSuspended`, as games' videos are, under the settings, a details screen or options panel and the power menu), muted, through `Grid/CardVideo`: a thread of its own decodes a few frames ahead with Core's `IVideoDecoder` (A2), and the main thread uploads the frame due, allocating nothing. Its clock is the focused clip's position: when the clip loops, the video seeks back to its first frame (a shorter video holds its last frame until then, a longer one is cut), so it keeps step with the model. A video that starts while its clip is under way (as the settings close, the clip having played on under them) seeks to the clip's position and drops the frames already past until one is due, rather than playing them fast to catch up (2026-11-09). A card without a focused clip loops it on its own clock. The decoding thread is background work that calls into Godot, so the app waits for it as it quits (A1 Boot, `GodotWork`, 2026-10-11). A system's details (`Screens/ModelView`, from 2026-10-27) play it too: their one-cell grid has the same decoder, and its card is focused with its clip playing, so the video keeps step there as in the grid, while the grid's is held off under them (one decoder at a time, as before, when the grid's played on under the details). Where there's no decoder (Linux), the card shows its texture. The Console theme's Amstrad CPC is the first (`videos/amstradcpc.mp4`); from 2026-10-27 its Game Boy, Game Boy Color, Game Boy Advance, Game Gear, PSP and Commodore 64 play their screens that way too (`videos/<id>.mp4`), from 2026-10-28 its PlayStation Vita, from 2026-11-02 its Flash card's monitor and its DOS, Windows 3.x, Windows 9x and Windows cards' monitors, and from 2026-11-08 its Vectrex's tube, the game's overlay sliding in and out in its video, and its Amiga's monitor. Media Foundation ignores an MP4's edit list, so a video with B-frames shows each frame up to two frames after its time on Windows (FFmpeg reads the list): the theme's videos are encoded without them from 2026-11-08 (`tools/console-models/card_video.py`; those encoded before still lag), and THEMING.md says to. Measured in movie-mode captures, the video's frame also comes a frame after the model's at 60 frames a second.
- **Game videos (2026-10-11):** a game template can play the focused game's own video (its `video` media, A4) on one of its slot faces, with the video's sound. `[templates.<id>] video` names the slot and `video_delay_ms` (0 to 60000, default 1000) how long the game is focused first; both are read like `fit` (a bad value is an error at its key and the template is kept without it, or with the base's, which an extension inherits; a slot the model lacks, or a delay without a video, is a warning), and carried on `GameTemplate` (`VideoSlot`, `VideoDelayMs`) to `ItemTemplate` (`GameVideoSlot`, `GameVideoDelay`; never for a per-game model or a card). The games source reads games' videos only when a template the theme uses plays them (`SlotLayout` adds the `video` kind to the media query), into one `MediaRef` per game (`IGridSource.TryGetVideo`). The games grid (`ItemGrid.UpdateGameVideo`, once a frame) counts the focused cell's time and, past the delay, opens the video through `Ui/VideoPlayback` (the details screen's player: its own decoding thread, the sound through an `AudioStreamGenerator` that also clocks the picture), on the grid's own `AudioStreamPlayer`; an ended video is opened again while its last frame holds, and takes over with its first. Unlike a card's video it can't swap a texture parameter: every game drawn with the template shares its material, so it would show on them all. The item shader instead has a `video_frame` sampler and a `video_state` (the cell, the slot, the picture's aspect and its fade) per material: only the fragment of that cell's slot face mixes the video over its art (fading in over 0.25 s), fitted like the slot's art (`video_whole` from the template's `fit`), so the template stays batched, the other games keep their art, and a `video_state` cell of -1 costs one comparison. Moving the focus, a launch, the grid hiding, a theme change or anything on the UI layer (`VideosSuspended`: the settings, a game's details or options, the power menu) stops it and its sound, and the art shows again; coming back starts the delay again. Memory Card's `crt_tv_90s` (from `Tools/CrtTvBuilder`) is the first, offered per game and system but assigned to none. Benches: [perf/game-videos.md](perf/game-videos.md).
- **The base theme (2026-10-03):** `memory-card` isn't a theme to choose but the base every theme builds on, loaded first (`ThemeCatalog.LoadBase`) and passed to every other theme's loader. What a theme leaves out is the base's: each look block, `look_transition_ms`, and, through the resolution order (A7), each system's template, card and colour. A theme's `game_template` (in `[defaults]` or `[systems.<id>]`) may name the base's templates as well as its own (`GameTemplate.ThemeId` says whose; the model's path is then the base's folder). A `[templates.<id>]` with a base template's id extends it key by key: `model`, `shape`, each slot's chain and each slot's `fit` it omits are the base's (`ModelFromBase` when the model is), and `model` is required only for a new id; a bad model leaves the extension out, so the base's template is used. A candidate's key includes the declaring theme, so an extension (the base's file, other chains) isn't merged with the base's template.
- **Validation:** like config (A5), a syntax error or an unsupported `format` rejects the theme (the default theme, `slab`, is used, else the base alone), and every other problem is a diagnostic naming the file, line, column and key: `user/themes/neon/theme.toml:12:1: error: templates.box.model: 'box.glb' doesn't exist in the theme's folder`. A bad look block falls back to the base theme's; a template whose model is missing or isn't a `.glb` inside the folder is left out, and whatever named it falls through to the next model in line; a template id neither the theme nor the base has is an error with "did you mean" over both; a bad slot chain falls back to the slot's default chain; unknown keys are warnings with "did you mean". A user theme's models are inspected (their material names, from the GLB's JSON chunk) when the manifest loads; the app's own themes' are checked by Core's tests against the repo's files instead (`ModelInspectorTests` runs every committed model through the inspector), and at load time.
- **Locations (2026-10-03):** the app's own themes (`memory-card`, `console` and, from 2026-10-04, `slab`, and from 2026-10-19, `classic-view`) live in the `themes/` folder beside the executable, outside the PCK, and are read with .NET file I/O like a user theme (in an editor run, `godot/themes/`). `godot/themes/` has a `.gdignore`, so Godot neither imports nor packs it (no "keep file" imports any more), the export preset excludes it, and the `odyssey_export` editor addon (`godot/addons/odyssey_export/`, `ThemesExportPlugin`) replaces the export's `themes/` with a copy when an export ends; `bench-export.ps1` and the release workflow fail an export without it. User themes are in `ConfigDir/themes/<id>/`; one with an app theme's id replaces it, but `memory-card` is reserved (a user theme with that id is ignored with a warning). Without the base theme the app can't start: the boot error names the folder. `[display] theme` in settings.toml picks the theme (`console` by default; `memory-card` there is a warning, and `console` is used); `--theme=<id>` overrides it for a run, and may name `memory-card`, the base alone, for captures and benches.
- **Switching:** the settings screen (M7: Menu, then Appearance, Theme) lists the themes by name (never the base), applies the one chosen without a restart, and writes `[display] theme`. T on the keyboard (systems screen) loads the next theme in id order for the session only (M6), as the nav script's `theme` step does.
- **Downloading themes (2026-10-16):** the settings' "Download themes" (under Look) offers the themes on a list the owner keeps, as ES-DE's theme downloader does, and installs each as a user theme. Core's `Theming/Downloads` does the work; the app's `Settings/ThemeDownloader` runs it.
  - **The list** is a git repository, [`GeekRhapsody/odyssey-themes`](https://github.com/GeekRhapsody/odyssey-themes) (`ThemeDownloads.DefaultListUrl`): `themes.json` and each theme's screenshots. It's cloned into `CacheDir/themes-list/` and, each time the page opens, fetched and reset to its branch (a change made to the clone is discarded, as in ES-DE); offline, last time's copy is shown with why it couldn't be refreshed. That's the only network the downloader uses unasked, and there's none at boot.
  - **`themes.json`** (format 1; comments and trailing commas are allowed, as the owner edits it by hand): `latest_app_version` (an older launcher says a newer one is out), and per theme `id` (its folder: a valid theme id, never a built-in theme's), `name`, `author`, `description`, `url` (an https git repository; a local list may name `file://` ones), `version` (shown only), `min_app_version` (an older launcher shows the theme but won't install it), `new`, `deprecated` (shown faded, "leaving the list soon"), `covers` (optional: what it brings, ES-DE's variant and colour-scheme counts: `system_cards`, `templates`, `menus`, `card_videos`, `game_videos`, as `odyssey-scrape inspect-theme --json` counts them; a theme without it is listed the same, and its details have no "What it brings" row) and `screenshots` (`image`, a path in the list, and `caption`). A bad entry is left out with a warning naming it; a file that can't be read shows no themes. A `commit` (lists from before the change below) is ignored.
  - **Each theme follows its repository's default branch, as in ES-DE.** The owner reviews a theme before listing it (its pull request to the list); after that, its author's pushes reach users as updates, unreviewed: keeping the theme working is the author's job (the owner's choice, 2026-10-16). Whether a theme has an update is known only by fetching it, which A on an installed theme does, as in ES-DE.
  - **Installing** clones the repository into `ConfigDir/themes-staging/<id>/` (beside the themes folder, so moving it in is a rename, but outside it, so an interrupted clone is never loaded) without checking anything out, turns the clone's own `core.autocrlf` off (so the files are what was committed, whatever the user's git settings), checks out its default branch's tip, loads the manifest over the base theme (refused only if it can't be used at all: a launcher too old for it, a damaged download, or a broken change its author pushed), then moves it to `ConfigDir/themes/<id>/`, where `ThemeCatalog` finds it like any user theme. A folder of that id the downloader didn't make (not a clone of the listed repository: ES-DE's manual download and wrong URL) or an interrupted one (a clone with no files: ES-DE's corrupt repository) is replaced only when the user says so, after being moved to `DataDir/theme-backups/<id>-<local time>/`. ES-DE renames such a folder `_DISABLED` in place, which would still be a valid theme id here. The staging folder is cleared each time the page opens.
  - **Updating** fetches and, when the branch has moved on, resets the clone to its tip; files git doesn't track stay, and a clone left detached (as the first version of the downloader left them) is put back on its branch. With nothing new (the clone is at the tip, or ahead of it with commits of the user's own), nothing changes, the user's own edits included, and the page says it's up to date. When an update would overwrite changes of the user's own (edited files, or commits the branch doesn't have), `ThemeChangesException` comes back and the page asks first, as ES-DE does. A new version whose manifest can't be used is refused, and the clone reset to the commit it was at, so the user keeps a working theme until its author fixes it. **Removing** deletes the folder (libgit2 leaves its objects read-only, which Windows won't delete, so the flag is cleared first). The theme in use can't be removed (choose another first), as in ES-DE.
  - **The page:** each theme's first screenshot (decoded on a worker), name, author and state (not installed, new, installed, the theme in use, changes of the user's own, another theme in its folder, an interrupted download, needs a newer launcher, leaving the list), and what A does (Get, Replace, or for an installed theme, Installed or In use: A then fetches its updates). A installs, replaces (asked first), or updates an installed theme (asking first when that would overwrite the user's own changes, else saying it's up to date); X opens the details (description, version, the commit installed, what it brings when the list says, the repository and folder, the screenshots full screen); Y removes it (asked first). One operation runs at a time, with a progress card (libgit2's objects received); after an install a question offers to use the theme, and installing or updating the theme in use reloads it (`SettingsController.ThemeUpdated`, `Navigator.ReloadTheme`).
  - **Costs:** LibGit2Sharp and its native libgit2 (`git2-*.dll`, 2 MB, in the export's data folder) load the first time the page reads an installed theme's clone, never at boot (checked on the export: neither is loaded until the page opens). Start-up on the export was 271.7 ms before and 280.7 ms after (medians of 5; runs ranged 263–383 ms in both; process start to interactive 1118 and 1110 ms), with the same frames.
  - **For the list's owner:** `odyssey-scrape inspect-theme [--json] <folder>` checks a theme as the app loads it and its models against their budgets, and prints its `covers`; `odyssey-scrape themes list|install|update|remove` does the downloader's work from the command line; `--themes-list=<folder>` (app and CLI) reads a list from a folder as it is, to test one before publishing it (`tests/themes-list/` is a made-up one for captures).
- **Menus (2026-10-15):** `[ui.menu]` sets the menus' colours and font: everything on the UI layer (the settings, the item options, the system and game details, the power menu, dialogs, the on-screen keyboard, the pickers, the media viewer's bar) and the job cards. From 2026-10-18 the text over the grids (`InfoOverlay`, `TitleList`) follows the same three colours through `OverlayPalette` (`Legacy`, the launcher's own exactly, unless the theme sets a colour, else `Derive`d: the hints are the derived menus' `Dim`, and the focused label's glow in the grids with labels lies between the background and the accent; the heading's subtitle and the list's text lie between the text and the background, and the list's highlight between the background and the accent, at Legacy's fractions); the heading's title stays white. It's applied in the navigator's constructor (before the first frame) and in `Navigator.ApplyTheme`, and skipped when the palette is the same; the status indicators and the titles printed on cards keep the launcher's look. Each key is the theme's, else the base's (memory-card sets none), else the launcher's own (`ThemeMenu`): background `#090D29`, text `#EEF1FA`, accent `#8FB2FF`. A theme that sets no colour gets the launcher's own palette exactly (`MenuPalette.Legacy`, M7's values), so its menus don't change by a pixel; one that sets any gets `MenuPalette.Derive`: rows, cards and the text fields' border between the background and the accent at Legacy's steps (hovered, focused, pressed), frames, fields and the dimming behind a menu the background darkened, dim and faint text between the text and the background. Two roles are equal in a derived palette exactly where they are in Legacy (a few alphas are 0.01 apart for that, on a black background), since a switch maps each colour shown to its role's new one (A1 Ui, `UiStyle.Apply`); Good, Warning and Bad don't change. A bad colour is an error at its key, and the base's is used. `font` must be a TTF, OTF, WOFF or WOFF2 inside the folder, at most 4 MB (`ThemeMenu.MaxFontBytes`; a font for Latin text is well under it, a CJK one isn't), or it's an error and the default font stays; a file Godot can't read as a font is a warning when the theme is applied. Characters the font lacks are drawn in a system font, as with the default font. Costs, on the export ([perf/menus.md](perf/menus.md)): nothing per frame (a style box or label costs the same whatever its colour; the 60 s scroll's frames, allocation and memory are unchanged); a theme switch that changes the menus' palette, with the settings open, makes one frame 2–4 ms longer (18–20 ms at 60 Hz; nothing when the palette is the same), because `UiStyle.Apply` sets every colour silently and has each resource, and the Godot `Theme`, tell its controls once; a font is read and parsed on a worker (3–8 ms for a 220 KB TTF), set in under 0.1 ms, and switching to it made no frame over 18 ms. The Slab theme (built in) sets grey menus (`#141414`, `#ECECEC`, `#C8C8C8`) and a black to dark grey background, with no font. From 2026-11-10 the menus' shapes are the same in every theme (`UiStyle`): panels with 6 px corners and no border or shadow (the job cards keep a 10 px shadow, having no dimming behind them); rows, cards and frames with 4 px corners, a plain row transparent, the focused one a flat fill (`RowFocus`) with white text and no outline or glow, and the details screens' big text block a lighter one (`RowHover`); only a text field has a border (`FieldBorder`, 1 px); titles have no glow, and section headings are in sentence case, in the dim colour at 16 px. Over the grids, the heading's title and the centre message have a dark shadow, the list layout's highlight is a flat fill and the showcase's screenshot frame has no border; the grids with labels keep the focused label's glow, the only thing showing their focus.
- **Motion (2026-10-15):** `[motion]` scales the grids' procedural motion (A7 Procedural fallbacks), as multiples of the launcher's own: `focus_sway` the focused item's sway about its vertical (0.3 + 0.3 sin(1.1 t) radians at 1, so 0 faces the camera), `idle_bob` how far the items not focused bob (0.012 of a model's height at 1, so 0 holds them still). Each is the theme's, else the base's (memory-card sets none), else 1 (`ThemeMotion.Default`); 0 to 3, and a bad one is an error at its key, and the base's is used. Clips still replace the motion they stand for (a `focused` clip the sway, an `idle` clip the bob). The sway is one more multiply in `ItemGrid.FocusTransform`, which already ran each frame for the focused item; the bob is the item shader's `idle_bob` uniform (default 1), set on a grid's materials only when the theme's isn't 1 (`ItemGrid.SetMotion`, at boot, on a theme switch, and for a system's details' `ModelView`), so no shader variant, no new per-frame code, and nothing at start-up beyond reading two keys. Measured on the export, before and after, with nothing else running ([perf/theme-motion.md](perf/theme-motion.md)).
- **Later:** `[ui]` is also reserved for a theme's sounds. The navigation sounds (2026-10-10) are the app's own for now, not a theme's.

## A7. glTF model spec

| Rule | Value |
|---|---|
| Format | glTF 2.0 binary `.glb`, one scene, everything embedded. Images are PNG or JPEG. |
| Units and axes | glTF defaults, with no conversion: metres, right-handed, +Y up, the front facing +Z (towards the viewer). |
| Origin | The bottom-centre of the rest-pose bounding box. The model stands on y=0, and spin is about +Y through the origin. |
| Size | The bounding box fits x∈[-0.5,0.5], y∈[0,1], z∈[-0.5,0.5], with the largest side about 1 m. <br>The loader fits the rest-pose bounding box to the grid cell by scaling a **wrapper node**, so animation clips are never touched. <br>Anything off by more than 10× gets a "wrong units?" warning. |
| Media slots | A material whose name is a media kind (`cover`, `back`, `spine`, `box_texture`, `label`, `screenshot`, `logo`, `hero`) is a slot, and shows that game's media through its fallback chain (below). <br>Every slot is optional, `cover` included (M6 part 2). <br>Matching ignores case and a trailing Blender `.NNN` suffix. <br>TEXCOORD_0 spans 0..1 across the face, upright, and sampling is clamped. <br>Art is centre-cropped to fill. The face's aspect ratio comes from the material's `extras.aspect`, or else from the slot mesh's bounds. <br>The material's own base colour texture is the chain's last fallback. <br>Set the base colour factor to white. |
| Materials | Remapped onto the launcher's one item shader, so a model never adds a shader variant. <br>Honoured (M6): base colour factor and texture (at most 4 textures a model, mipmapped when loaded), roughness, and vertex colours (multiplied into the base colour). <br>Planned, ignored until then: metallic, normal, emissive, `KHR_materials_unlit`, `KHR_texture_transform`, alpha and double-sided. <br>A model that requires an extension outside `KHR_materials_unlit`, `KHR_texture_transform`, `KHR_materials_emissive_strength`, `KHR_mesh_quantization` and `KHR_lights_punctual` (Draco, for one) is rejected. |
| Animations (optional; names match like slots) | `idle` loops while not focused. <br>`focused` loops while focused. <br>`launch` plays once; the emulator starts when it ends or after 2 s, whichever comes first. <br>Node TRS, skinning (≤64 joints, 4 or 8 influences per vertex) and morph targets (≤8) are supported. Other clips are ignored. <br>Matching also ignores an object name before a `|` (Blender's `Armature|idle`); the loader renames each to its canonical name. <br>Drawing (M6 part 2): a model with an `idle` clip is drawn as a node in every cell (so keep idle clips for system models); one with only `focused` or `launch` clips stays batched, and the focused cell swaps to a node while it plays them, blending (0.25 s) to a `_rest` clip made from their tracks before it swaps back. |
| Procedural fallbacks | The grid's focus lift and scale always apply. <br>Spin or bob runs only for a state without a clip. <br>The launch fallback spins up and moves towards the camera. <br>A theme scales the focused sway and the idle bob (A6 Motion, 2026-10-15). |
| Ignored | Cameras, `KHR_lights_punctual` and extra scenes. Theme lights are the only lights. |
| Runtime handling | A user's `.glb` (per-game, `ConfigDir/models/`, a user theme's) is first processed by Core (`ModelInspector`, `ModelProcessor`: checked, textures over the budget's side scaled down, rejected if broken or more than 2× over) and cached in `CacheDir/models/`, keyed by path, size, time, kind and processing version; a rejection is cached and logged too (`DataDir/logs/models.log`). Then every `.glb`, the app's own themes' included (read from its `themes/` folder without that processing), is parsed by `GltfDocument` on a worker and converted there, textures and meshes created there too; the main thread adopts the result within 2 ms a frame. Every model is fitted to the spec (standing on y = 0, centred, largest side 1 m). A per-game model shows its system's template until it has loaded, is fitted into the cell, and is drawn on the cell's own node. Users import per-game models with `ModelImportService` (a `.glb`, or an OBJ model converted by `ObjConverter`). |

### Budgets

M6 part 2 enforces them (`ModelBudget`); the per-game one was measured with 300 per-game models in a 3,000-game library ([perf/m6-models.md](perf/m6-models.md)).

| Model | Triangles | Textures (excluding media slots) | Materials |
|---|---|---|---|
| Game template (built-in or per system) | ≤ 2,000 | ≤ 2 × 1024² | ≤ 4 |
| Per-game custom model | ≤ 5,000 | ≤ 2 × 1024² | ≤ 4 |
| System model | ≤ 30,000 | ≤ 4 × 2048² | ≤ 8 |

Textures are the base colour images the materials use. Normal, metallic-roughness, occlusion and emissive maps aren't drawn yet, so they don't count (their references are still checked); they will once they're honoured.

`ModelInspector` checks each model first:
- Over budget: the model loads, with a warning.
- More than 2× any count (triangles, textures, materials; 64 joints a skin and 8 morph targets a mesh are budgets too): the model is rejected, and the next model in the resolution order is used.
- A texture over the budget's side is scaled down when the model is processed, so its size never rejects a model.
- From 2026-10-08, `ModelConverter` draws every model texture at most 1024 on its longer side (`MaxTextureSide`): halved with `Image.ShrinkX2` (a 2×2 box filter) while it's twice that, then Lanczos for the rest. Textures are uploaded uncompressed (RGBA8 with mips: an export can't compress at run time), so the console theme's 2048-pixel images took 137 MB of video memory; at 1024 they take 37 MB, with no difference seen at 1080p, in the grid or the details' view.
- A broken file (a bad range or index, a node loop, a non-PNG/JPEG image, data outside the file) is rejected with its reason, before Godot parses it.
- Every warning and rejection goes to `DataDir/logs/models.log`, with what's used instead; `odyssey-scrape models-log` prints it, and `inspect-model` checks a file.

### Media slots and fallback chains (M6)

Each slot of a game template has a **fallback chain**, walked for every game when its cell binds:
- A media kind: the game's media of that kind, if it has one. Any image kind can fill any slot (a back slot can show a screenshot).
- `generated`: a face the launcher draws (below), for `cover`, `back`, `spine` and `label` only.
- `authored`: the material's own texture, as the model was made (or its base colour).

The first media kind the game has wins; `generated` or `authored` ends the walk; and every chain ends, implicitly, with `authored`. While the media loads, the chain's next `generated` or `authored` entry shows, and the media fades in over it. If the media has no usable derivative, the walk carries on from the next entry. A slot the theme doesn't list is `[<its kind>, "generated"]` where the launcher can draw it, else `[<its kind>]`. A system card's only media is its theme's logo for the system (A6 System cards' logos, from 2026-10-04): its chains are the theme's `system_slots` (only `logo`, `generated` and `authored`), and a slot they don't list is `generated` where possible, else `authored`. The systems grid streams logos through a `TextureStreamer` of its own (one worker, cells as the systems pool, `CacheDir/theme-logos` as its cache), whose arrays exist only while a card chain names `logo` (`ThemeRuntime.CardLayout`); for any other theme it's never asked for anything. A card's `ModelCandidate` carries its chains (`CardSlots`), and its key includes them, so two systems sharing a model but not its chains are two grid templates.

```toml
[templates.showcase_case.slots]      # the test theme's template
cover = ["cover", "generated"]
spine = ["spine", "logo", "generated"]
screenshot = ["screenshot", "hero", "authored"]   # "authored": the model's own test card
```

When a game's media changes (a rescan finds new or removed art or models, a scrape saves media, a game is cleared, or derivatives are baked), the library raises `MediaChanged`, and the shown list's media is read again: each changed game's cell walks its chains again, keeping its model and every slot whose media is unchanged.

### Resolution order (first hit wins)

User files are matched by `path_key`, with or without the ROM's extension, and found by folder convention: per-game models are indexed by the scanner (media kind `model`), and the two per-system folders are listed when the theme loads, so nothing is probed per item. A candidate that fails to load or is rejected by its budget is skipped for the next one.

- **Game** (M6):
  1. `DataDir/media/<system>/models/<name>.glb` (the media folder, A4)
  2. the template the user chose for the game (`userdata.db` `game_overrides.template`, from 2026-10-07): that template id in the active theme, else in the base theme (neither: the next level, and the game's options say so)
  3. `ConfigDir/models/templates/<system>.glb`
  4. the user's `game_model` in systems.toml: that template id in the active theme, else in the base theme (a warning if neither has it)
  5. the active theme's `[systems.<system>] game_template` (its own template or the base's, from 2026-10-03)
  6. the active theme's `[defaults] game_template` (likewise)
  7. the base theme's `[systems.<system>] game_template`
  8. the base theme's `[defaults] game_template`
- **System card:**
  1. `ConfigDir/models/systems/<system>.glb`
  2. the active theme's `[systems.<system>] model`
  3. the active theme's `[defaults] system_model`
  4. the base theme's (`models/systems/generic.glb`)

  Favourites, Recently played and All games go through the same levels with `<system>` as `favourites`, `recently_played` and `all_games` (from 2026-10-11, All games from 2026-10-15; before, they always had the default card, levels 3 and 4).

From M7 part 2 the user sets levels 1 to 4 in the app: a game's own model, or (from 2026-10-07) one of the active theme's or the base theme's templates, in its options (Model), and a system's own card, own template or `game_model` in the system's options. The game's model swaps in place (its media changed); a system's changes reload the theme in the background.

**A game's chosen template (2026-10-07)** is an ordinary grid template, batched in its MultiMesh like a system's, not a per-game node. The slots a grid streams (`SlotLayout`) come from its loaded templates, so the templates games chose load with the theme: `ThemeRuntime.UseGameChoices` takes the ids (`GetChosenGameTemplatesAsync`, one `SELECT DISTINCT`) at boot, once the library is open (the theme resolves before it, so the runtime collects again if it was ready; with no choices nothing changes), and on every theme switch or reload. A game's row carries its choice (`GameRow.Template`), and `GamesSource.BindTemplates` gives each game its chosen template's index when the theme has it loaded, else its system's. Choosing a template the grid already has (a system's, or another game's choice) rebinds the shown list (`ThemeRuntime.UseGameChoice`, then the games' rows read again); another loads the theme again in the background, as a system's choice does. Choosing a template deletes the game's own model, which would still win (as a system's choice deletes its own template); importing a model keeps the choice, which shows again if the model is removed. Clearing the game keeps it, as it keeps the emulator.

User models (levels 1 and 3) use the default slot chains; a chosen template (levels 2 and 4) uses its theme's. The base theme's templates (M5), which every theme can name, are `dvd_case` (GameCube; PlayStation 2's until 2026-10-18, the Wii's, Wii U's, Xbox's and Xbox 360's until 2026-11-10), `jewel_case` (Dreamcast (NA/JP), Mega-CD (JP) and the other CD systems; Saturn's until 2026-10-02, Dreamcast's until 2026-11-02 and the Mega-CD's until 2026-11-03), `psx_jewel_case` (PlayStation: from 2026-10-08), the jewel case sized by ScreenScraper's PAL art (a 680 × 680 front, a 765 × 680 back and a 65 × 680 spine) so none of it is cropped, 140.43 × 125 × 13.4 mm, `dreamcast_jewel_case` (Dreamcast: from 2026-11-02; below), `megacd_case` (Mega-CD) and `segacd_case` (Sega CD: both from 2026-11-03; below), `cartridge_box` (NES, N64, Game Boy Advance), `gameboy_box` (Game Boy, Game Boy Color, Super Game Boy: from 2026-10-01), `generic_box_spine` (DOS) and `generic_box_logo` (Windows, Steam: the game's logo along the spine), two templates of one PC big box, `generic_box.glb` (shaped by each game's art: below; 190 × 240 × 50 mm without art; from 2026-10-02, renamed from `big_box`), `generic_box_top_spine` (N64: from 2026-10-03), the same box landscape with its spine on the top and bottom, `generic_box_top.glb` (190 × 136 × 30 mm without art; the left and right sides are plain case), `snes_box_horizontal` (SNES: from 2026-10-04), the US and European cardboard box, landscape with its spine on the top and bottom and its left and right flaps plain dark case (190 × 139 × 28 mm), and `snes_box_vertical` (from 2026-10-04: no system's, a user choosing it for a game, in its options, from 2026-10-07; the Super Famicom's, `sfc`'s, from 2026-11-03), the Super Famicom's, portrait with its spine on both long sides and its top and bottom flaps plain off-white (99.6 × 180 × 29.1 mm), both fixed shapes sized by ScreenScraper's art (a 680 × 497 front with a 680 × 97 top, and a 478 × 864 front with a 136 × 864 spine) and printed over their bevels, `clamshell` (32X, SG-1000 and SuperGrafx; Master System's until 2026-10-18; from 2026-11-10 shaped by each game's art, below, its insert folding round a spine with sharp edges, 136 × 190 × 24 mm without art) and the same case in white, `clamshell_white` (Nintendo DS, 3DS and Wii), teal, `clamshell_teal` (Wii U), and Xbox green, `clamshell_green` (Xbox and Xbox 360: all three from 2026-11-10), `megadrive_clamshell` (Mega Drive: from 2026-10-09; below), `ps2_clamshell` (PlayStation 2: from 2026-10-18; below) and `ps2_clamshell_blue` (no system's: chosen per game or system), `psp_clamshell` (PSP: from 2026-10-18; below), `umd_case` (N-Gage; PSP's until 2026-10-18), `switch_case` (Switch: from 2026-10-07; below), `flat_cover` and `flat_screenshot` (from 2026-10-07: no system's; below), `crt_tv_90s` (from 2026-10-11: no system's; below) and `arcade_cabinet` (from 2026-10-02: Arcade, MAME, FinalBurn Neo, the CPS boards, Neo Geo, NAOMI, Atomiswave, Model 2 and 3, ST-V, Triforce, Daphne, and the console and PC arcade systems; below), plus the generic system model, a memory-card-shaped slab with a `label` slot, in [`godot/themes/memory-card/models/`](../godot/themes/memory-card/models/). `BoxTemplateGenerator` builds them from real case sizes in millimetres: a rounded-rectangle front outline extruded to the depth, with bevelled edges, one surface per material (the front slot, `back`, `spine`, `case`; the generic box's front and back chamfers are part of the `cover` and `back` slots, showing the art's edge, and its case is dark, so no light band frames its art), and `extras.aspect` on each slot (the spine's is its straight wall: the depth less the chamfers, over the height less the corners). The front and back art spans the whole outline, so the chamfers hide a bevel's width of it, except on a spec with `ArtInsideBevels` (`psx_jewel_case` and the clamshells), whose art spans the caps inside them, with each face's aspect the cap's. A spec with `SharpSpineEdges` (the clamshells) has no chamfers between the spine and the front and back: the spine is one flat face the whole depth, its aspect the depth over the height less the corners, and the caps reach it, so the cover's, spine's and back's art meet at sharp folds. The top and bottom chamfers run out round the spine's corners, whose radius is the bevel's, so the caps' corners there are square and the spine's art is as tall as the cover's. The rim round the top, the bottom and the opening side stays case. It exports them with Godot's glTF exporter; the output is deterministic, and `BuiltInModelTests` checks each file against this spec. About 220 triangles each.

**The arcade cabinet (2026-10-02).** `ArcadeCabinetBuilder` builds the top half of an upright cabinet, cut off below the control panel, from a real one's proportions in millimetres: side panels (blue outside, with a grey T-moulding along their front edges so the black cabinet's outline reads on a dark background), a marquee box whose front panel is the `label` slot (2.7:1, taller than most real marquees, since logos are drawn whole and most are nearer 2:1), the monitor tilted back 12° in a framed bezel as the `screenshot` slot (568 × 426 mm, 4:3, nearly filling the bezel, with `extras.aspect` set, as the face is tilted; matte, roughness 0.9, as glossy glass mirrored the lights over the focused cabinet's screen), and a control panel with two players' joysticks and six buttons each. The side panels' lower part is inside the control panel, 10 mm or more under its top and behind its front: where they shared its front plane and stood a few millimetres proud of its top, their grey edges flickered as the model moved. The theme gives it `label = ["logo", "generated"]`: the logo drawn whole over a deep shade (below), else a printed title; and `fit` `screenshot = "whole"`, so a vertical game is pillarboxed on the screen's own switched-off texture, which also shows for a game with no screenshot (the default chain). It's 1,402 triangles and three materials: the two slots, and `case`, whose many colours come from a 64² palette texture with each part's UVs in the middle of its colour's 16-pixel swatch (one colour at any distance, as the UVs don't vary across a triangle; vertex colours would do it too, but their colour space through Godot's glTF round trip is one more thing to get wrong). No clips, so it stays batched.

**The television (2026-10-11).** `CrtTvBuilder` builds a 1990s colour CRT, about a 21-inch set, in millimetres: a charcoal cabinet 600 × 495 mm with rounded corners (40 mm, six segments each) and an 8 mm chamfer round its front, running straight back 110 mm, then the tube's housing tapering to a 440 × 380 back with eight vents on its top; a swivel base under its middle; under the screen a silver badge, a speaker grille each side (six slots) and a control flap with the power button, a red standby light and four small buttons. The screen is the `screenshot` slot, 444 × 334 mm (4:3, `extras.aspect` set, as it's curved), a 16 × 12 grid bulging 14 mm in the middle and flat at its edges (z = bulge × (1 − a²)(1 − b²), smooth normals from its slope), set 15 mm back behind a recess whose walls slope from a 462 × 352 opening to a 440 × 330 back edge: the recess hides the screen's outer 2 mm, which rounds its corners as a tube's mask does, and with a flat edge (rather than a sphere's cap) no part of the screen comes through the walls. Its authored texture is the arcade cabinet's switched-off tube, matte. It's 782 triangles and two materials, the screen and `case` (a palette texture, as the cabinet's). The theme gives it `fit` `screenshot = "whole"` and `video = "screenshot"` (A6 Game videos). No clips, so it stays batched.

**The game cases built in Blender** (`megadrive_clamshell`, `ps2_clamshell` and `ps2_clamshell_blue`, `psp_clamshell`, `dreamcast_jewel_case`, `megacd_case` and `segacd_case`, `switch_case`): how each is built, its measurements and its references are in [tools/console-models/README.md](../tools/console-models/README.md), "memory-card's game case templates".
**The Mega Drive clamshell (2026-10-09).** `megadrive_clamshell` is Sega's plastic case (the European Mega Drive's and the US Genesis's) as one solid, built in Blender by `tools/console-models/mega_drive_clamshell.py` rather than by `BoxTemplateGenerator`, whose extruded outline can't make its rims or its tab. Its outer hull follows a reference model of the case: a flat spine wall; on the top, the bottom and the opening side, the two halves' walls set back 1 to 1.8 mm between a 1.2 mm rim round each face, with a seam where they meet; and the shop hanger tab, the back's rim carried 15.4 mm above the top, with its hook hole (a slot with a round notch over its middle). Nothing inside is modelled. Its paper insert is sized by ScreenScraper's scans, not a ruler: a 484 × 680 front and back and a 96 × 680 spine make it 131 × 184 × 26 mm, and 3 mm of the black case shows round it at the top, the bottom and the opening side, as on a real case, so the case is 134 × 190 × 26 mm (205.4 mm high with the tab). The `cover`, `back` and `spine` slots are the insert's faces (the front's and back's reaching round the 0.6 mm rounded edges to the spine), their UVs projected flat across the insert; the rest is black `case`. Every face is flat-shaded, as the generated boxes are: smooth normals bent towards the rounded edges spread over the long triangles across a face and showed as hot spots. 602 triangles, four materials, no texture.
**Flat cards (2026-10-07).** `FlatBuilder` builds two cards with no thickness, each a quad with a media slot on its front and plain dark `case` on its back, both in the plane z = 0 (4 triangles, two materials; the front fully matte, roughness 1, so it reads as the image rather than a print under a glossy sleeve). Both have `shape = "media"`: `flat_cover` is the `cover` slot, 3:4 without art, and takes each game's cover's proportions; `flat_screenshot` is the `screenshot` slot, 4:3 and near-black (its authored texture) without art, and having no `cover` material takes each game's screenshot's proportions. As every vertex has z = 0 and the shader moves a vertex by the sign of its z, the depth a scraped spine gives them moves nothing, and the cards stay flat. No clips, so both stay batched; no system uses them, and users choose them per game or per system.

**Generated faces (M5; `generated` in a chain from M6).** With front art only, the item shader makes the rest. The spine is the cover's dominant colour, with the title reading top to bottom and a darker band where a logo would be; on a spine face wider than high (a box's top or bottom, from 2026-10-03) the title reads left to right and the band is at the left end.

**Art drawn whole (2026-10-02).** When a slot's chain resolves to the `logo` kind, or its template's `fit` sets it to `"whole"`, the grid sets a bit in the slot's state (and another for a logo), and the shader draws the art whole over the slot's fallback using its alpha. Art the template fits whole fills the face's width or height at its own proportions (a tall screenshot pillarboxed), with no margin. A logo is drawn within a margin, over that fallback: the cover's dominant colour (the game's plain colour when the template shows no cover) darkened to a deep shade (a `generated` fallback), or the authored texture. On a `spine` it's turned to read top to bottom like the generated title (at most 90% of the length and 76% of the depth), unless the face is wider than high (a box's top), where it stays upright in the same margins; on any other slot it's upright (88% of the width, 84% of the height), as on the arcade cabinet's marquee and the sample TV's plate. Before the arcade cabinet only a spine did this, and other slots cropped a logo like any art, which cut it off and showed its transparent parts as whatever colour they hid. Every other kind crops to fill, as before. The back is a darker gradient of that colour, with the title and a strip of the cover. The dominant colour is chosen per vertex from the cover's 4×4 mip, as the texel most like the others, preferring saturated ones. With no art, the front is the plain colour (the system's colour, varied per game) with the title and a thin frame. Titles come from the title atlas.

### Boxes shaped by the art (2026-10-01)

A template with `shape = "media"` (A6) takes each game's shape from its art, so a game's cover, back and spine show whole whatever their proportions (DOS big boxes vary from game to game). The built-in `generic_box_spine` (DOS), `generic_box_logo` (Windows, Steam), `generic_box_top_spine` (N64), and `clamshell` (32X, SG-1000, SuperGrafx), `clamshell_white` (Nintendo DS, 3DS, Wii), `clamshell_teal` (Wii U) and `clamshell_green` (Xbox, Xbox 360: all four from 2026-11-10) do it. The depth still comes from the game's `spine` image when the spine shows its logo.
- **The rule** (`Theming/BoxShape`, pure): the front is as wide over high as the game's `cover` media (that kind itself, not whatever a chain resolved to; for a model with no `cover` material, from 2026-10-07, its `screenshot` media: `ItemTemplate.ShapeSlot`), clamped to 1:4–4:1, with the larger side filling the list's envelope. The depth comes from the `spine` media: one taller than wide is the box's side, and the depth is its width over height (clamped to 0.02–0.5) times the box's height; one wider than tall is the box's top (from 2026-10-03: US and European SNES and N64 boxes, 680 × 97 against a 680 × 497 front), and the depth is its height over width (clamped likewise) times the box's width. The template's own spine face says only where the spine is drawn: one wider than high (`extras.aspect` above 1) is a top and bottom, and its reshaped aspect is the box's width over its depth. Scraped images are normalised (ScreenScraper's are 700 pixels high), so only ratios are read. Without a cover or a spine, the rest shape's proportions stand in.
- **The envelope:** when a list is bound, the grid walks it once on the main thread (only if a template it uses has the shape: 2.7 ms for 10,000 games in a Debug build, [perf/box-shapes.md](perf/box-shapes.md)) for the widest and tallest box, each with its larger side 1, and sizes the cells to that instead of the template's rest size. A list of tall boxes keeps tight columns. A box whose cover arrives later is scaled down into the envelope until the list is bound again.
- **Reshaping, in the item shader:** each cell's growth goes in two slot-state columns when it binds or its media changes (half the width's growth, the height's, half the depth's, the height of the top half's start; then the front and spine faces' aspects, and which slot is the front). The vertex shader moves every vertex of each half of the rest mesh out (or in) by the growth, so bevels and corners keep their size and flat faces their normals, and the `cover`, `back` and `spine` faces take the new aspects. Every other cell's columns are zero, and the shader leaves those vertices alone. The box stays in its template's MultiMesh: no mesh per game, no node, nothing allocated. The template mesh's custom bounds cover the largest box (sides of 1, half as deep), for culling.
- **Limits:** the model must keep vertices off the centre planes (x = 0, z = 0, half the height), which the generated boxes do; sides never shrink below 0.1 (depth 0.01) of the cell. A model with clips (a node tree) or a per-game model keeps its own shape.


## Decisions log

The console theme's cards' build records (each card's construction, its clip, its fixes and the tooling they share) are in [tools/console-models/HISTORY.md](../tools/console-models/HISTORY.md), not here.

| Date | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| 2026-09-27 | Stack: Godot 4 .NET (C#), Tomlyn, Microsoft.Data.Sqlite (WAL, versioned migrations), glTF `.glb` only, platform code behind interfaces. | The owner decided it. Don't revisit without asking. |
| 2026-09-27 | TFMs follow Godot's generated csproj: net8.0 for 4.7.2. Tests use `RollForward=Major`. | The owner's rule. This machine has no .NET 8 runtime, and the editor already rolls forward to .NET 10. |
| 2026-09-27 | **Open risk (closed 2026-11-09: net10.0, below):** .NET 8 support ends on 10 November 2026, and exports bundle the runtime they target (confirmed: .NET 8.0.31, self-contained). M1 benches a net10.0 build; switching needs the owner's approval. | Godot keeps a higher TFM if one is set; it only raises TFMs below net8.0. |
| 2026-09-27 | The 1 s start-up target covers **our code only**: `app_startup_ms`, from the first autoload's `_EnterTree` to `interactive`. Engine and .NET start-up is reported separately. `DebugHooks` stays the first autoload. | The owner clarified this. Engine and runtime start-up isn't ours to optimise beyond renderer and publish settings. |
| 2026-09-27 | Windows export preset: ExportRelease, separate PCK, S3TC/BPTC textures, `modify_resources` off, console wrapper for debug exports only. `tools/bench-export.ps1` starts the exe directly and reads its JSON. | This is enough for benchmarking. An embedded icon and version info can come later. |
| 2026-09-27 | `.slnx` solution, with Godot's three configurations. | .NET 10 default. Godot 4.6+ finds `.slnx` files; its builds use the csproj directly. |
| 2026-09-27 | xUnit v3 on Microsoft.Testing.Platform (`global.json` test runner). The VSTest packages are kept for IDE test explorers. | xUnit v3 4.x can't use VSTest mode under the .NET 10 SDK. |
| 2026-09-27 | `path_key` (lower-invariant NFC `rel_path`) is the identity everywhere, including `userdata.db` and media file names. | Survives case-only renames, keeps `Game.cue` and `Game.chd` apart, and makes keys portable. |
| 2026-09-27 | Per-game overrides (title, emulator, hidden) live in `userdata.db`. TOML holds system-level intent. | High-cardinality user data that can't be rebuilt. |
| 2026-09-27 | User model and art overrides are picked up by folder convention, and the scanner indexes them. | No per-item probing at runtime, and no config keys for thousands of games. |
| 2026-09-27 | Background: a canvas layer, not a sky shader. Lights are in view space. | No radiance or cubemap passes, free cross-fades, and exact colours (verified). |
| 2026-09-27 | `library.db` keeps versioned migrations, and rebuilding is only the fallback. | The owner decided on versioned migrations. The design review suggested rebuild-only. |
| 2026-09-27 | `.bat` and `.cmd` emulator targets are rejected. | cmd.exe re-parses arguments, so `&` and `%` in ROM names would break or inject commands. |
| 2026-09-27 | Forward+ with D3D12, set explicitly in `project.godot`. | What 4.7's project manager writes for new projects; the engine's fallback is Vulkan. M1 compares the two. |
| 2026-09-27 | UK English for docs, UI text, our identifiers and our config keys. External names keep their spelling. | Owner's locale and the organisation's standard. |
| 2026-09-27 | **Renderer: Mobile on D3D12**, replacing Forward+. `project.godot` switches when the real grid lands (M5). | M1: the same frame pacing, 36–42% less GPU time at 1280×800, the same or faster start-up, and output within 4/255. The design uses no Forward+-only feature. ([SPIKE_RESULTS.md](SPIKE_RESULTS.md) §3) |
| 2026-09-27 | **Item rendering:** one `MultiMesh` per template plus a `Texture2DArray` with a layer per pool cell, indexed through `INSTANCE_CUSTOM`. Per-node instances only for per-game custom models. The camera scrolls and the grid stays still. | M1: both approaches hold vsync with 64 cells. MultiMesh uses 3 draw calls against 42 and about 20% less render CPU, and its pool memory is fixed (§1). Nodes-only is the fallback. |
| 2026-09-27 | **Cover derivative: BC7 DDS, 512², full mips**, loaded with `Image.LoadDdsFromBuffer(span)` from a pooled buffer. BC1 is optional for low memory. | M1: 0.26 ms to decode against 4–5.4 ms for JPEG and PNG, 4× less VRAM, and the grid textured in 10 ms against about 100 ms. KTX, `.res` and raw were measured and rejected (§2). |
| 2026-09-27 | **Open risk (closed by M4: our own encoder, below):** the export templates have no BCn encoder (`Image.Compress` returns `Unavailable`). M4 picks an encoder: a package (needs approval), our own, or a GPU compute port. JPEG derivatives are the fallback. | Measured in the export (§2a). JPEG met every frame target at 4× the VRAM. |
| 2026-09-27 | **Uploads:** pool textures are created at boot and only updated on the main thread (4 MB per frame). No texture is created on the main thread while browsing. 2 decode workers. | M1: a BC7 layer update takes 0.03 ms. Main-thread creation has 17–66 ms outliers. Creation on a worker doesn't stall the main thread, but blocks the worker for about 12 ms per call. 4 workers didn't help (§2d). |
| 2026-09-27 | **Targets:** the hitch target is reworded as "no hitches caused by the launcher" (measured against a no-texture control, 0 frames over 2×, 0 hitches fullscreen). New targets: textured ≤ 100 ms, ≥ 99% textured while scrolling, working set ≤ 512 MB, pool ≤ 64 MB. Main-thread allocation ceiling: 4 KB per 60 s scroll. **These wait for the owner's sign-off.** | M1: a periodic present delay about every 5 s, outside our code, gives 0–2 hitches per 60 s run in windowed mode even with nothing streaming. The other targets were met with headroom. |
| 2026-09-28 | Packages: Tomlyn 2.10.1 and Microsoft.Data.Sqlite 10.0.12 (with SQLitePCLRaw's bundled `e_sqlite3`), in Launcher.Core only. Both target net8.0. | Part of the decided stack; M2 adds them. Tomlyn 2.x reads TOML 1.1 only. |
| 2026-09-28 | Config is mapped by hand from Tomlyn's syntax tree into a tree that keeps positions. No reflection mapping, no snapshot cache. | Diagnostics must name the file, line, column and key after merging, and only the syntax tree has positions. A warm load is 2 ms ([perf/m2-core.md](perf/m2-core.md)). |
| 2026-09-28 | `Diagnostic` gains a `Key` (dotted path), and prints as `file:line:column: severity: key: message`. A syntax error reports only its first parser error. | Errors must name the key. Tomlyn's recovery reports follow-on errors that aren't real. |
| 2026-09-28 | **Several ROM folders per system:** `rom_dirs` (an array) replaces `rom_dir`. `library.db` gets a `rom_dirs` table and `games.dir_id`. `path_key` stays unique per system across its folders; the earlier folder wins. | The owner asked for several folders per system. Keeping the folder out of the identity means moving ROMs between drives keeps their user data. |
| 2026-09-28 | Multi-file games: `.cue` and `.gdi` hide their tracks, as `.m3u` hides its discs. Parsed playlists are cached in a `playlists` table keyed by size and mtime. Disc systems' default extensions leave out `.bin`. | The owner's grouping rules. The cache keeps an unchanged rescan from reading 1,578 playlists (65 ms with it, against 104 ms for the scanner alone without it). |
| 2026-09-28 | File-name tags are stored in their own columns (`region`, `languages`, `revision`, `disc`, `tags`). The sort key is internal: lower-case, accent-free, leading The/A/An dropped, natural numbers, discs right after their game. `game_overrides` stores the override's sort key too. | Region and revision stay available for scraping and display. A stored key lets SQL sort the grid by plain binary comparison, with no collation or C# re-sort. |
| 2026-09-28 | Readers come from our own pool, with `userdata.db` ATTACHed once and `PRAGMA query_only`, opened read-write rather than read-only. | Microsoft.Data.Sqlite's pool would keep ATTACHes and file handles we can't control during a rebuild swap. A read-only open of a WAL DB can fail when its `-shm` file doesn't exist yet. |
| 2026-09-28 | Built-in emulator profiles use `{retroarch}` (default `C:/RetroArch-Win64`) and `{emulators}` (default `C:/Emulators`), defined in the default `settings.toml`. Their command lines haven't been launched yet; M3 checks them. | Every built-in system needs a valid emulator for config to validate. |
| 2026-09-28 | A scan removes the games of a folder that's gone (an unplugged drive). User data stays. | `library.db` stays a pure function of config and disk, so a rebuild equals an incremental scan. |
| 2026-09-28 | Scan benchmarks run in the normal `dotnet test`, in a non-parallel collection, with budgets at 2–3× the Debug measurements, capped by the M2 targets (ROADMAP.md M2 log). | They take about 12 s, mostly writing the 10,000 files, and catch regressions early. |
| 2026-09-28 | **Network shares:** folders are listed with a 256 KB buffer (not .NET's 4 KB), and systems are scanned 8 at once on dedicated threads. | On a NAS over SMB, an unchanged rescan of 9,422 games took 13.6 s, almost all of it listing folders. The buffer halves the round trips, and parallel systems hide the latency. Locally it costs nothing ([perf/m2-core.md](perf/m2-core.md#network-share-added-2026-09-28)). Dedicated threads, because blocking a dozen thread-pool threads on I/O would stall other work while the pool grows. |
| 2026-09-28 | **Scan exclusions are configurable:** `[scanning] exclude` in `settings.toml` applies to every system, before each system's own `exclude`. The default is `["images", "manuals", "videos", "gamelist.xml"]`. | The owner asked for a configurable list rather than hard-coded folders. ES-DE keeps media inside each system folder: on the NAS, those were 83% of the entries listed. With the three changes, an unchanged rescan there takes 1.4 s. |
| 2026-09-28 | **Launch placeholders (M3):** `{rom_name}` is renamed `{rom_stem}`, and `{emulator}` and `{core}` are added. A profile's RetroArch core is its own `core` key (a path, expanded like `executable`), and the built-in RetroArch profiles pass it as `-L {core}`. | The owner's placeholder set. A `core` key lets config load check that the core exists, and keeps the args readable. Nothing had shipped, so the rename needs no alias. |
| 2026-09-28 | **Missing installs are warnings, checked at config load**, naming the affected systems, for emulators that enabled systems use. The executable is checked, and the core only if the executable exists. | The owner asked for load-time validation that reports affected systems. The wait also polls the job every second. `Process.Start` isn't used on Windows. | `Process.Start` can't put a child in a job from its first instruction, and assigning it afterwards races with stub launchers. Building the command line ourselves means quoting it ourselves, by the C runtime's rules (A5), which the tests check against `CommandLineToArgvW` and a real child process. |
| 2026-09-28 | **Launch outcomes:** one game at a time; events `Starting`, `Running`, then `Exited` or `Failed`, on worker threads. A non-zero exit within 5 s is `Failed` and isn't counted as a play. | 2026-10-11 | **Background work that calls into Godot goes through `Boot/GodotWork`, and the app waits for it as it quits.** A job calls `TryEnter` before its first Godot call and `Exit` after its last; a long-lived worker holds it for its whole run and stops when it sees `Quitting`. `Main._ExitTree` (the scene tree going, before the engine shuts down) calls `Quit`, which refuses new jobs, so a job queued but not started never begins, and waits up to 5 s for the running ones, logging the wait. Through it: each model's parse and conversion (`ModelLoader`), the boot's media arrays, `Thumbnails` (the pickers', the media panel's, the details screen's), the match panel's covers, and the decoding threads of `VideoPlayback` and `CardVideo` (replacing the card video's own wait). The texture streamers' decode threads were already joined by their `Dispose`. | A quit before the models had loaded crashed the process in the interop (an access violation in whatever call the worker was in: `GltfDocument`, an `Image`, an `ArrayMesh`), since a worker on the thread pool carried on calling into Godot while it shut down: 17 of 24 captures that quit in the first 22 frames (editor build, the console theme), every one in a model's conversion. After: none of 24, waiting 14 to 203 ms, and none of 16 that quit while the CPC's video played (13 to 19 ms: its thread looks every 20 ms) or later. A count with a refusal, rather than cancelling each job, covers work started anywhere with one rule, and needs nothing from Core, whose work never calls Godot. Waiting is on the main thread, which A3 forbids per frame, but only once, as the app quits. |

A card's way (pointing the material's `authored_N` at the video) can't work for games: every game drawn with a template shares its material, so all of them would show the focused game's video, and the screen is a slot, not an authored texture. Two uniforms per material (the frame, and which cell and slot show it) keep the template batched and need no variant; the comparison is all the other faces pay. The sound and the picture's clock come from the details screen's `VideoPlayback`, so a loop opens a new playback while the last frame holds rather than seeking (its sound reader can't seek). The delay is per template, the owner's choice; it also means scrolling never opens a decoder. Benches ([perf/game-videos.md](perf/game-videos.md)): frames unchanged; while a 640 × 480 video plays, render CPU +0.17 ms, working set +53 MB, video memory +3 MB; on a system without the TV, before and after are the same. |

The 20–30 MB left above a fresh Slab start (10 MB on Vulkan) is neither textures nor buffers (Godot's total less both), most likely blocks the GPU memory allocator keeps once it has them, which the app can't free (not confirmed); it doesn't grow with more switches. Applying a theme takes about the same time on the main thread (2–10 ms), and the collection doesn't block it. |

Of the helper programs, Native AOT (the owner's choice) is C# like the rest, about 1.8 MB and needs no .NET on the PC; a self-contained single file would add about 30 MB to the zip, and a C++ helper a second language and build. Replacing whole top-level entries (the data folder, `themes/`) drops files an older version had and the new one doesn't, and keeps a portable install's `userdata` and `portable.txt`, which a release never contains (a package that did is refused).
